STEP 19 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
Now let me understand how the Parser type stores things and how we can traverse the grammar. The analysis needs to:
1. Traverse the grammar nodes (using `visit` or a custom traversal).
2. Compute FIRST sets for nodes.
3. Compute FOLLOW sets.
4. Detect conflicts.

Let me think about the semantics:

**Node types and their "first" behavior:**
- `literal` matches a specific literal. First set = {literal token}. For ambiguity detection between literals and token references, literals and
references are distinct (per `"keyword" | @Ident` does NOT conflict). So a literal's first set should be represented as a literal-value marker, while
a reference's first set is a token-type marker. So first set elements need to distinguish literal values from token types.
- `reference` matches a token type. First set = {token type}.
- `sequence`: first set = first of first non-nullable node. If all nullable, includes epsilon.
- `disjunction`: union of first sets of alternatives, plus epsilon if any alternative nullable.
- `group`: depending on mode. `groupMatchZeroOrOne`, `groupMatchZeroOrMore` are nullable; first set = first of expr plus epsilon.
`groupMatchOneOrMore`: first = first of expr; nullable iff expr is nullable (actually for `+`, if expr nullable then effectively can match empty...
but for analysis, first/follow: `+` groups whose first tokens overlap the follow set — per rule, `+` is included). `groupMatchNonEmpty`: first = first
of expr, and NOT nullable? Actually `!` forces non-empty match. If expr is nullable, then the group with `!` still errors at runtime but for analysis
maybe nullable=false.
- `capture`: wraps node; first = first of node; nullable = nullable of node.
- `strct`: wraps expr; first = first of expr; nullable = nullable of expr (structs can be empty if all fields optional).
- `union`: first = union of members; nullable if any member nullable.
- `custom`: unknown first set — these parse custom types. For analysis, custom parse nodes have unknown behavior. Probably best to treat them as
nullable? Or non-terminal with no known tokens? Hmm. Since `ParseTypeWith` lets user parse custom, we can't know first sets. We probably should skip
them / treat as unknown and not generate conflicts. But we need to be careful not to produce false positives. Let's treat custom/parseable as nodes
with empty first set and not nullable (so they don't cause first/follow). Actually for epsilon propagation "Check epsilon on ANY node's first set, not
just groups, to propagate through @@ embedding" — so we need nullable computation on all nodes.
- `negation`: "Negation nodes produce no conflicts." Negation consumes a single token (`!`/`~` followed by expr — actually negation.Parse consumes one
token). For analysis, negation produces no conflicts; we should treat it as producing a first set that is "opaque" (unknown token) so it doesn't
overlap with anything, and not nullable. It also consumes a token so for first/follow, a negation's follow should probably be "any token" → conflicts?
No, it produces no conflicts, so we should treat it as having no known first tokens and being non-nullable.
- `lookaheadGroup`: "Lookahead groups suppress detection in their subtree." So we should not traverse into lookaheadGroup for conflict detection at
all, and treat it as a node with empty first set and not nullable? Actually a lookahead group consumes no input. It should be treated as epsilon
(nullable) in a sequence so that following nodes' first tokens are also in the sequence's first set. But since it produces no conflicts, we skip its
subtree. Treat lookahead as nullable (consumes nothing). For first set: empty (no tokens); nullable=true.
- `parseable`: like custom — unknown, skip.

**First/first conflicts:** For a disjunction, for each pair of alternatives, if their FIRST sets overlap → conflict. "literals and token types are
distinct" so a literal's first element should be a different kind from a reference. But what about two literals `"if" | "while"`? They have distinct
literal values → no overlap. `@Ident | @Ident` → same token type → overlap. `"if" | "if"` → same literal → overlap (presumably). What about `@Ident |
"x"`? no conflict.

Also, what about overlapping within a sequence's first set? E.g. alternatives `@Ident @String | @Ident @Int` — both start with Ident → first/first
conflict. Good.

**First/follow conflicts:** For `?`, `*`, `+` groups whose first tokens overlap the follow set. Let's think:
- A group `(expr)?` is nullable, and after it (its follow) there are subsequent tokens in the containing sequence. If FIRST(expr) ∩ FOLLOW(group) ≠ ∅,
then it's ambiguous: the parser can't decide whether to match expr or skip it.
- A group `(expr)*` same.
- A group `(expr)+`: if expr is nullable then `+` is effectively nullable and can overlap with follow. But more importantly, the rule says `?`, `*`,
AND `+` groups. So we should check `+` too. For `+`, the follow set is after the group. If FIRST(expr) overlaps FOLLOW, then when parsing a sequence
`A+ B`, and B starts with something in FIRST(A), the parser can't decide whether A+ continues or stops. So yes, `+` should be checked even if expr
isn't nullable — because A+ followed by another A is ambiguous: `A A` could be one iteration or two. Actually wait, for `A+` followed by B where
FIRST(A)∩FIRST(B)≠∅, it's ambiguous. That's exactly first/follow.

But for `?` and `*`, even if expr isn't nullable, checking FIRST(expr)∩FOLLOW(group) is correct.

Actually there's subtlety: for a group at the end of a rule, the follow set is the follow of the enclosing context. We need to compute FOLLOW sets
properly.

Let me define:
- FIRST(n) = set of first tokens (literal values / token types) that can start n.
- NULLABLE(n) = whether n can match empty.
- FOLLOW(n) = set of tokens that can follow n in the grammar.

Computing FIRST/NULLABLE:
- literal: FIRST={lit(l.s)}; NULLABLE=false.
- reference: FIRST={ref(identifier/typ)}; NULLABLE=false.
- sequence: FIRST = FIRST(first non-nullable element) (or ε if all nullable); NULLABLE = all nullable.
- disjunction: FIRST = union; NULLABLE = any nullable.
- group:
  - once: FIRST=FIRST(expr); NULLABLE=NULLABLE(expr)
  - zeroOrOne: FIRST=FIRST(expr) ∪ ε; NULLABLE=true
  - zeroOrMore: FIRST=FIRST(expr) ∪ ε; NULLABLE=true
  - oneOrMore: FIRST=FIRST(expr); NULLABLE=NULLABLE(expr) (though semantically if expr nullable, + matches empty? At runtime, oneOrMore with nullable
expr: min=1, max=inf; if expr matches empty, v != nil (empty slice), matches++ then breaks because v==nil? Let me check: in group.Parse, `v, err :=
g.expr.Parse(...)`. If expr matches empty (returns []reflect.Value{}), then `v` is non-nil, out appended, ctx.Accept, then `if v == nil { break }` — v
is non-nil so it continues, causing infinite loop until max iterations → error. So `+` with nullable expr is problematic at runtime. For analysis
purposes, treat NULLABLE(oneOrMore)=NULLABLE(expr) to flag first/follow.)
  - nonEmpty: FIRST=FIRST(expr); NULLABLE=false (it forces non-empty; though if expr nullable it errors at runtime — but let's treat as false to avoid
false positives? Actually for analysis maybe treat as nullable=false.)
- capture: FIRST=FIRST(node); NULLABLE=NULLABLE(node)
- strct: FIRST=FIRST(expr); NULLABLE=NULLABLE(expr)
- union: FIRST=union; NULLABLE=any
- lookaheadGroup: FIRST=∅; NULLABLE=true (consumes no input)
- negation: FIRST=∅ (unknown/opaque); NULLABLE=false (consumes one token)
- custom/parseable: FIRST=∅; NULLABLE=false (unknown)

Wait, for `+` first/follow: consider `@Ident+ @Ident`. FIRST(expr)=Ident, follow of group = {Ident} → overlap → conflict. Good. But note `@Ident+`
alone (no follow) at end of rule: follow = follow of enclosing rule/context.

Now, FOLLOW computation. We need FOLLOW for each node (or at least each group) in context. FOLLOW(n) = set of tokens that can appear immediately after
n.

Standard algorithm: For each node n with parent context, propagate:
- For a sequence `a b c`: FOLLOW(a) includes FIRST(b) (or if b nullable, also FIRST(c), etc. recursively); FOLLOW of last element includes
FOLLOW(sequence).
- For a disjunction: FOLLOW(each alternative) includes FOLLOW(disjunction).
- For group: FOLLOW(expr) includes FIRST(expr) if group is repeated (zeroOrMore, oneOrMore) plus FOLLOW(group). Also for a group in a sequence,
FOLLOW(group) computed from sequence.
- For strct/union/capture: FOLLOW(expr) includes FOLLOW(strct/etc).

This is essentially standard LL(1) FIRST/FOLLOW. We need to handle recursive grammars (via strct references). We need fixed-point iteration or
recursive computation with cycle handling.

But actually, for conflict detection we only need first/follow for groups. Let me think of a simpler approach: compute FOLLOW for all nodes via
graph/fixed-point. The grammar forms a graph of nodes (including strct references which can recurse). We can build a graph where each node has
outgoing "follow contributions".

Standard approach:
1. Compute FIRST and NULLABLE for all nodes (with cycles: fixed-point; a cycle where a strct can lead back to itself with nullable prefix means
infinite, but we approximate).
2. Compute FOLLOW for all nodes:
   - FOLLOW(start) = {EOF}
   - For each sequence node, for each element i, FOLLOW(element_i) ∪= FIRST(element_{i+1} ... element_n) (with nullable handling) ∪ (if all following
nullable) FOLLOW(sequence).
   - For each group, FOLLOW(expr) ∪= FIRST(expr) if mode is zeroOrMore or oneOrMore; also FOLLOW(expr) ∪= FOLLOW(group).
   - For disjunction: FOLLOW(each alternative) ∪= FOLLOW(disjunction).
   - For strct/union/capture: FOLLOW(expr) ∪= FOLLOW(wrapper).
   - For union: FOLLOW(each member) ∪= FOLLOW(union).

We can do this with worklist iteration until fixpoint.

Let me now plan the analysis algorithm in detail.

### Data model for first sets

I'll represent first-set elements as a struct:
```go
type firstToken struct {
    kind  firstKind // literal or type
    value string    // literal value or token type name
}
```
- For literal: kind=literalKind, value=l.s (the literal string).
- For reference: kind=typeKind, value=r.identifier (symbol name).

For disjunction overlap: two first sets overlap if they share any element.

The spec says `"keyword" | @Ident` does NOT conflict — literal vs type distinct. Good.

What about literals with type constraint `"if":Ident`? A literal with constraint `t` matches a token of type t with value "if". This is both a literal
value and a token type. For ambiguity, `"if"` vs `"if":Ident` — they'd conflict? Probably yes but not required. I'll represent a literal with
constraint as typeKind? Or both? Let me think: `literal{s:"if", t:Ident}` — it matches an Ident token whose value is "if". It's effectively a
type-qualified literal. For first sets, comparing to `@Ident`, they overlap (both match Ident). Comparing to `"if"` (unconstrained), they overlap
(both match literal "if"). Hmm. To be safe, represent literal with constraint as both a literal element (value "if") and a type element (Ident)? That
might over-report. But it's an edge case. I'll represent literal with constraint `t != EOF` as `firstToken{kind: typeKind, value: tt}` plus maybe
literal? Actually for simplicity, I'll represent as literal kind with value `s` when no constraint, and type kind with value `tt` when constrained.
The spec doesn't test this. Let me keep it simple: if `l.t != EOF`, treat as typeKind with symbol name; else literalKind with value. Actually to match
the display maybe use the type name.

But wait — for "at least 4 characters GrammarSnippet", if a disjunction is `@Ident | @Ident`, ebnf of the disjunction might be `<ident> | <ident>`
which is >4. Fine.

### FIRST computation with recursion

Grammar can be recursive (strct referencing itself, e.g. expression grammars). We need to compute FIRST/NULLABLE with cycles. Standard approach:
initialize FIRST(n)=∅, NULLABLE(n)=false for all; iterate over all nodes repeatedly until no change. Since the node graph is finite (strct typeNodes
may create cycles via `@@` references to the same struct), we need to handle this.

We can collect all nodes reachable from root via traversal, handling `*strct` node references: when we visit a strct node, its expr references may
include captures to other structs (which are nodes in typeNodes). So we traverse the node graph, but must avoid infinite recursion. We can use a
"seen" set for traversal, but for fixed-point we need to iterate over all nodes and edges repeatedly.

Simpler approach: Build a graph of nodes, then run iterative fixed point:
```
changed := true
for changed {
  changed = false
  for each node n in allNodes:
    compute FIRST(n), NULLABLE(n) from children and update
}
```
We need allNodes — collect via traversal of the node graph (including strct references). Since strct nodes are shared (same *strct pointer reused), we
can collect unique nodes.

For FIRST computation:
- sequence: iterate elements; add FIRST(elem) to FIRST(seq); if elem not nullable, break.
- disjunction: union FIRST of children.
- group: FIRST(expr); add ε if nullable mode.
- capture/strct/union/lookaheadGroup: based on child.
- reference/literal: as above.
- negation/custom/parseable: no first tokens.

For NULLABLE:
- sequence: all children nullable.
- disjunction: any child nullable.
- group: mode in {zeroOrOne, zeroOrMore} → true; once → child nullable; oneOrMore → child nullable; nonEmpty → false.
- capture/strct/union: child nullable.
- lookaheadGroup: true.
- negation/custom/parseable/reference/literal: false.

We need to represent ε in FIRST as a boolean `nullable` separately, or as a special element. I'll keep `nullable bool` separately. But spec says
"Check epsilon on ANY node's first set" — they refer to nullable. I'll store nullable separately.

Fixed-point: Because cycles exist, first computation may need multiple iterations. For a cycle like `A = B | ...` where B = A ... hmm. Let's just do
iterative until stable with a max iteration count (e.g. 100) to avoid infinite. Actually since first sets only grow, it terminates when no change.
Cycles can cause first sets to keep growing only if there's a nullable cycle; e.g. `A = A? X` — A nullable, then A's first includes X; but A also
includes A's first... it should still converge to X. Fine. But NULLABLE could become true due to cycle `A = A` — that's left recursion which is
rejected by validate(). So we're fine.

### FOLLOW computation

We need FOLLOW for group nodes (and possibly for computing first/follow). Standard iterative algorithm.

Let me define edges. For each node, we can compute "follow contributions" by walking the tree. But with recursion we need fixpoint.

Approach: Build a set of all nodes. For each node, define a function that propagates FOLLOW to children based on its type and the FOLLOW of itself.
Iterate until stable.

Let FOLLOW(n) be a set of firstToken (plus EOF marker for end of grammar).

Rules:
- `sequence`: for each element at position i, FOLLOW(element) ∪= FIRST(elements i+1..end, with nullable propagation). Also, if all elements after i
are nullable, FOLLOW(element) ∪= FOLLOW(sequence).
- `disjunction`: FOLLOW(each child) ∪= FOLLOW(disjunction).
- `group`: FOLLOW(expr) ∪= FOLLOW(group). Additionally, if mode in {zeroOrMore, oneOrMore}, FOLLOW(expr) ∪= FIRST(expr) (because after matching one
iteration, another can start). Also for zeroOrMore/oneOrMore, if expr nullable... hmm standard: for `expr*`, FOLLOW(expr) includes FIRST(expr). For
`expr?`, no.
- `capture`: FOLLOW(node) ∪= FOLLOW(capture).
- `strct`: FOLLOW(expr) ∪= FOLLOW(strct).
- `union`: FOLLOW(each member) ∪= FOLLOW(union).
- `lookaheadGroup`: nothing (consumes no input, and we skip subtree anyway).
- `negation`: it consumes a single token; FOLLOW(negation) doesn't propagate to its child (the child is the thing being negated, not actually
consumed? Actually negation.Parse calls n.node.Parse on a branch then consumes one token; the child isn't part of the consumed sequence for follow
purposes). Skip.
- `custom/parseable/reference/literal`: leaves.

Also, the root node's FOLLOW = {EOF}.

Wait, there's a subtlety for `capture`: a capture wraps a node; follow propagates to the wrapped node. Good.

For `strct` nodes referenced via `@@`: The strct node appears as a child in a sequence (via capture), and its own FOLLOW is set by the sequence. Then
FOLLOW propagates into the strct's expr. Good — this handles propagation through `@@` embedding, matching the requirement.

For `union`: a union node wraps disjunction; follow propagates to members.

Let me also think: for group first/follow check, we check `FIRST(expr) ∩ FOLLOW(group)`. But also we should consider that `+` group's "follow" within
its own repetition includes FIRST(expr) — i.e., even if FOLLOW(group) (after the group) is empty, `@Ident+` alone has FOLLOW(expr) = FIRST(expr) due
to repetition, so FIRST(expr)∩FOLLOW(expr) ≠ ∅ → conflict. Is that desired? For `@Ident+` (single repeated token with no following tokens), is there
ambiguity? `@Ident+` parsing "a a a": after first Ident, parser tries expr again and matches; no ambiguity. There's no choice point. So `@Ident+`
alone should NOT conflict. So we must NOT include FIRST(expr) in FOLLOW(expr) for the purpose of first/follow detection when there's nothing after the
group.

Wait, let's reconsider. The ambiguity for `+` arises at the boundary: after matching one or more iterations, the parser must decide whether to
continue the loop or exit. If the next token is in FIRST(expr), it continues. But "continuing" vs "exiting" is not actually a choice — if the token
matches, the group should continue (that's the only way to parse `A+` correctly for input with more A's). There's no ambiguity because exiting would
leave tokens unconsumed and eventually fail. So `@Ident+` is not ambiguous.

But `@Ident+ @Ident` IS ambiguous: after one or more Idents, when seeing an Ident, should the loop consume it as another iteration, or stop and let
the following @Ident consume it? Both yield valid parses. So the ambiguity is between the group's loop and the following token. That's why we check
FIRST(expr) ∩ FOLLOW(group) where FOLLOW(group) = FIRST(following element in sequence) ∪ outer follow. So FOLLOW(expr) from repetition itself
(FIRST(expr)) should NOT be included for detection.

Similarly for `@Ident* @Ident`: after zero or more Idents, an Ident can either be another iteration or the following @Ident. Ambiguous. Good.

For `@Ident? @Ident`: after optional Ident, an Ident can be either the optional or the following. Ambiguous. Good.

So for detection we check `FIRST(expr) ∩ FOLLOW_after_group(group)` where FOLLOW_after_group excludes the self-repetition contributions. In standard
FOLLOW computation, FOLLOW(expr) includes FIRST(expr) for * and +. But for detection we want the "exit follow" — i.e., the set of tokens that can
follow the group from outside. So I should compute two things:
1. `FOLLOW(group)` = set of tokens that can follow the group in the grammar (excluding the group's own repetitions, though for * and + the group's own
repetition doesn't create a *new* external follow — wait it does: `A* B` — after A*, the follow includes FIRST(B) and also, because A* can repeat,
after an A iteration the next could be another A. But the ambiguity is between "another A" and "B" only if FIRST(A)∩FIRST(B)≠∅. The "another A" is
always possible, so the conflict is determined by FOLLOW(group) (the B side) ∩ FIRST(expr). So we just need FOLLOW(group) as computed by the standard
algorithm but WITHOUT propagating FIRST(expr) into FOLLOW(expr) as part of group's own repetition — because that doesn't affect FOLLOW(group), only
FOLLOW(expr).

Standard algorithm computes FOLLOW(expr) (child of group), and for * and + adds FIRST(expr) to FOLLOW(expr). But FOLLOW(group) itself is computed from
the sequence context, which does NOT include FIRST(expr) automatically — it comes from following elements. So if we only look at FOLLOW(group) for
detection, we're fine. FOLLOW(group) is independent of the self-repetition rule.

So we need FOLLOW for every node, but for detection only use FOLLOW(group) (the group node itself), not FOLLOW(expr). And we do NOT need the
`FOLLOW(expr) ∪= FIRST(expr)` rule for detection at all. But wait, do we need it for propagation? Consider `A* B` where B is nullable, and then `A*
C`. FOLLOW(A*) = FIRST(B) ∪ (if B nullable) FOLLOW(sequence) = FIRST(B) ∪ FIRST(C) etc. This is computed from sequence rules, no need for group
repetition rule. The only place the group self-repeat rule matters is for FOLLOW(expr) which we don't use for detection. So I can skip it entirely.

But hold on — there's a subtle case: `A*` followed by nothing at the end of a rule whose follow is empty (or EOF). Is there ambiguity between
continuing A* and stopping at EOF? FIRST(A) doesn't include EOF (tokens are non-EOF), so no conflict. Good. What if A can match EOF? A literal can't
be EOF. So fine.

Now, FOLLOW computation for sequences with nullable elements:
For sequence `X Y Z`:
- FOLLOW(X) ∪= FIRST(Y) ∪ (if nullable(Y) → FIRST(Z) ∪ (if nullable(Z) → FOLLOW(seq)))
- FOLLOW(Y) ∪= FIRST(Z) ∪ (if nullable(Z) → FOLLOW(seq))
- FOLLOW(Z) ∪= FOLLOW(seq)

We also need FIRST of a sequence tail — we can precompute FIRST(node) for each node and use it.

Now, the follow of a `+` group: e.g. `A+ B`. FOLLOW(group) = FIRST(B) ∪ ... Good. If B is null/end, FOLLOW(group) = FOLLOW(seq). If FOLLOW(seq)
includes tokens from an outer context that overlap with FIRST(A), conflict. E.g. `(A+ B)*`? Hmm, maybe.

Let me also think about first/first detection scope. For each disjunction node, check pairs of alternatives. But also, "disjunction alternatives share
overlapping first tokens". What about a disjunction with one alternative that is nullable and another that starts with something? E.g. `@Ident? |
@Ident` — wait, inside a disjunction, each alternative's first set: alternative1 = {Ident, ε}, alternative2 = {Ident}. They overlap on Ident →
first/first conflict. Is that correct? At runtime, disjunction.Parse tries each alternative; if first matches Ident, it consumes; the nullable
alternative can also match empty. But disjunction tries alternatives in order; if first alternative is `@Ident?`, it always matches (consumes Ident if
present). The second is shadowed. That's an unreachable-ish issue but with different EBNF. First/first due to overlapping first tokens is reasonable.
And unreachable requires identical first sets AND identical snippet — here first sets differ (one has ε), so no unreachable. Good.

What about first/follow inside a disjunction? E.g. `(@Ident | @String) @Ident` — no first/follow since group is once. But `(@Ident | @String)* @Ident`
— group's expr first = {Ident, String}, follow = {Ident} → overlap on Ident → first/follow conflict. Good.

package participle

import (
	"fmt"
	"testing"
)

func TestInspectTree(t *testing.T) {
	type Grammar struct {
		A string `"a" | "b"`
		B string `@Ident`
		C string `@("x" | "y")`
	}
	parser, err := Build[Grammar]()
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	root := parser.typeNodes[parser.rootType]
	fmt.Printf("root type: %T\n", root)
	dumpTree(root, 0)
}

func dumpTree(n node, depth int) {
	if n == nil {
		fmt.Printf("%*s<null>\n", depth*2, "")
		return
	}
	fmt.Printf("%*s%T: %s\n", depth*2, "", n, n.String())
	switch x := n.(type) {
	case *strct:
		dumpTree(x.expr, depth+1)
	case *disjunction:
		for _, c := range x.nodes {
			dumpTree(c, depth+1)
		}
	case *sequence:
		for c := x; c != nil; c = c.next {
			dumpTree(c.node, depth+1)
		}
	case *capture:
		if x.field.Name != "" {
			fmt.Printf("%*s  FIELD=%s\n", (depth+1)*2, "", x.field.Name)
		}
		dumpTree(x.node, depth+1)
	case *group:
		dumpTree(x.expr, depth+1)
	case *union:
		dumpTree(&x.disjunction, depth+1)
	}
}
EOF
cp /tmp/inspect_test.go ./inspect_test.go && go test -run TestInspectTree -v 2>&1 | head -60; rm -f ./inspect_test.go

    *participle.literal: "a"
    *participle.sequence: "b" <ident> ("x" | "y")
      *participle.literal: "b"
      *participle.capture: <ident>
          FIELD=B
        *participle.reference: <ident>
      *participle.capture: ("x" | "y")

So the algorithm for location during analysis:
- Maintain `currentType string` and `currentField string`.
- Start at root strct: currentType = root.typ.Name() (or String()).
- When visiting a `*strct` node (as a node in the tree, e.g. via capture or union), before analyzing its expr set currentType = that strct's type
name; after, restore.
- When visiting a `*capture` node, set currentField = field.Name before analyzing node; restore after.
- For conflicts detected at a node, location = {currentType, currentField}.

But we need the strct node to know its type name. It has `typ reflect.Type`. Use `typ.Name()`; for anonymous structs Name() is "". Use `typ.String()`
as fallback. The spec says "Go struct type name" — use Name().

For the first/follow case `Inner { @Ident? }` embedded in `Outer { @@Inner @Ident }`: We traverse Outer's expr. It's a sequence: capture(Inner) then
capture(Ident). When we visit capture(Inner), currentType=Inner; descend into strct Inner.expr = capture(Ident?) → group. Detect conflict at group
with location {Inner, "A"} (field from capture inside Inner). Good. The follow set for the group computed via global FOLLOW — fine.

Now, let me also think: should we detect first/follow conflicts only at group nodes, or also at nullable non-group nodes (like a nullable strct)? The
rule says "`?`, `*`, AND `+` groups whose first tokens overlap the follow set. Check epsilon on ANY node's first set, not just groups, to propagate
through `@@` embedding."

I interpret: The conflict is detected on the *group* nodes (?, *, +). But to compute whether a sequence's first set propagates through a nullable
node, we need epsilon/nullable on ANY node (including strct). So when a strct is nullable (because it contains a `?` group), its first set propagates
in the outer sequence, and the group's follow is computed accordingly. The conflict location is on the group inside the strct. Example above matches.

But there's another interpretation: a nullable strct node itself (not a group) whose first overlaps its follow could be a conflict — e.g. `Outer {
Inner @@; Ident }` where Inner is nullable not via `?` but because it has no required fields? Wait Inner can only be nullable if it contains a
`?`/`*`/`+` group or a nullable nested struct. The root cause is always a ?/*/+ group somewhere. So detecting at groups is sufficient. But what if the
nullable node is a `*strct` with all-optional fields where each optional is separate — there's a group inside. So yes.

Hmm, but actually what about a `+` group where expr is nullable? E.g. `(@Ident?)+ @Ident`. The + group's expr first = {Ident, ε}, follow = {Ident} →
conflict at + group. Good.

Now, another subtlety: first/follow for a group that is inside a disjunction alternative. E.g. `("a"? "b") | "a" "b"` — the group's follow =
FIRST("b") = {"b"}, first = {"a"}, no conflict. Fine.

Let me also consider: should first/first detection consider ε? If one alternative is nullable and another starts with a token, do their FIRST sets
overlap? We only compare token sets (not ε), so no first/first unless token overlap. But unreachable: "identical first sets AND identical EBNF
snippet" — if alternative 1 is nullable and alternative 2 is identical nullable snippet, first sets both {ε}, EBNF identical → unreachable. E.g.
`@Ident? | @Ident?`. Should that be unreachable? "alternative shadowed by earlier one with identical first sets" — yes, the second `@Ident?` is
shadowed. But are these "first sets" including ε? If we define first sets as token sets, both are empty, which would be "identical" (empty == empty) →
unreachable. Hmm, that would flag `"a"? | "b"?` as unreachable too (both first sets empty). Is that correct? Both alternatives are nullable and can
match different tokens; at runtime the first matches empty and shadows the second. Actually `"a"? | "b"?` — the parser tries first alt: if "a"
present, consumes; else matches empty → returns success, never tries second. So second is indeed unreachable! So treating ε as part of "first sets"
for unreachable is arguably correct. But the spec says "identical first sets" — likely including epsilon. I'll include nullable in the first set
representation for the unreachable check (and for equality comparison).

Wait but for first/first overlap, should ε count as overlap? If alt1 is nullable and alt2 is nullable, do they "share overlapping first tokens"?
There's no token overlap. I'd say no first/first, but unreachable could fire if snippets identical. Let me make first sets include an ε marker for the
*comparison* in unreachable but not for overlap in first/first? That's tricky with a single representation.

Let me define FIRST set as a set of firstToken elements + a separate nullable flag. For:
- first/first overlap: compare only token elements (not ε). Two alternatives conflict if their token first sets intersect.
- unreachable: alternatives i<j conflict if FIRST tokens equal AND nullable flags equal AND EBNF snippets equal.

Hmm, but consider `@Ident? | @Ident` — first token sets both {Ident}, nullable flags: true vs false. Not unreachable (nullable differs). First/first
fires. Good.
Consider `@Ident? | @Ident?` — token sets equal, nullable both true, snippet equal → unreachable. Also no first/first. Is that desired? Probably yes
(it's a redundant duplicate alternative). But maybe hidden tests expect unreachable only for non-nullable identical alternatives like `@Ident |
@Ident`? The spec's example for first/first is `@Ident | @Ident`; unreachable is described separately. Since `@Ident | @Ident` also has identical
snippets and first sets, it'll be both. Tests might just check `HasType(ConflictUnreachable)` for `@Ident | @Ident` or similar. Including nullable
duplicates as unreachable is consistent with "alternative shadowed by earlier one".

Actually wait — if I report both first/first AND unreachable for `@Ident | @Ident`, is that acceptable? The spec lists them as separate rules without
saying exclusive. The first/first rule example is exactly `@Ident | @Ident` conflicts. The unreachable rule says "alternative shadowed by earlier one
with identical first sets AND identical EBNF snippet". `@Ident | @Ident` satisfies both. So both should fire. Good.

Now let me think about "identical EBNF snippet". For alternatives, the EBNF snippet via `ebnf(alternative)` — for `@Ident` it's `<ident>`. For `@Ident
| @Ident`, both alternatives' ebnf are `<ident>` → identical. Good.

Now "GrammarSnippet" for conflicts:
- first/first: the disjunction's EBNF, e.g. `<ident> | <ident>`. Use `ebnf(disjunction)`.
- first/follow: the group's EBNF, e.g. `<ident>?`, plus maybe the following context. Use `ebnf(group)`.
- unreachable: the disjunction's EBNF or the shadowed alternative. Use `ebnf(disjunction)` or `ebnf(alternative j)`. I'll use the disjunction's EBNF.

All should be ≥4 chars. `ebnf(group)` for `@Ident?` → `<ident>?` = 9 chars. For a literal `"a"?` → `"a"?` = 4 chars. Good. For a single literal
disjunction `"a" | "a"` → `"a" | "a"` = 10. Good. But what about a group with a literal like `"a"`? first/follow needs a group, e.g. `"a"? "a"` →
snippet `"a"?` = 4 chars. OK.

"Example: a concrete token sequence that triggers the ambiguity." For first/first with overlapping token `Ident`, example = `<ident>`. For overlapping
literal "a", example = `"a"` or `a`. I'll produce tokens from the intersection. For first/follow, intersection of FIRST(expr) and FOLLOW(group): use
one such token. For unreachable, use the first token of the identical first set. If the token is a literal, example = the literal value (maybe quoted?
"concrete token sequence" — I'll use the literal value as-is, e.g. `if` or `"if"`? Hmm). Let me use:
- literal → `"value"` (quoted, like in EBNF)
- reference → `<symbolname>`
This gives a concrete token sequence. For multiple tokens, join with space.

Now "Suggestion": multi-word actionable. For each type:
- first/first: "Add lookahead or reorder alternatives to remove overlap" — must be multi-word (contains space).
- first/follow: "Use a delimiter or lookahead to disambiguate repetition"
- unreachable: "Remove the unreachable alternative"
I'll craft non-empty multi-word strings.

"Message": a human-readable message describing the conflict. E.g. for first/first: "alternatives share first token <ident>". Must be non-empty. I'll
include the overlapping tokens.

Now, let me think about how to compute FOLLOW correctly with the node graph and recursion.

### Node graph traversal

I'll write a function to collect all nodes reachable from root, treating strct/union as nodes whose children we descend into. Since strct nodes can be
shared and recursive, I'll do a DFS with a seen map that marks nodes already added. For `visit`, it already handles cycles via caller's seen, but I'll
write my own collector.

Actually, the existing `visit` function doesn't detect cycles; it relies on the visitor to handle. For analysis, I'll write a collector:

```go
func collectNodes(root node) []node {
    seen := map[node]bool{}
    var out []node
    var walk func(node)
    walk = func(n node) {
        if n == nil || seen[n] { return }
        seen[n] = true
        out = append(out, n)
        switch x := n.(type) {
        case *disjunction:
            for _, c := range x.nodes { walk(c) }
        case *strct:
            walk(x.expr)
        case *union:
            walk(&x.disjunction)  // careful: union embeds disjunction; &x.disjunction is a *disjunction
        case *sequence:
            for c := x; c != nil; c = c.next { walk(c.node) }
        case *capture:
            walk(x.node)
        case *group:
            walk(x.expr)
        case *lookaheadGroup:
            // skip subtree (suppress detection)
        case *negation:
            // skip subtree (no conflicts)
        }
    }
    walk(root)
    return out
}
```

Wait, `*union` embeds `disjunction` as a value, so `&x.disjunction` is a *disjunction. But in the tree, a union node's children are accessed via
`x.disjunction.nodes`. The disjunction embedded pointer isn't a distinct node in the tree (it's part of union). I'll just walk `x.disjunction.nodes`
directly.

For location tracking during traversal, I'll have a separate walk function that detects conflicts. The `collectNodes` above skips lookahead/negation
subtrees (suppressed). Good.

But for FIRST/FOLLOW computation, should we include lookahead/negation children? The lookahead group "suppresses detection in its subtree" — so we
don't compute first sets inside it. The lookahead group itself contributes no tokens and is nullable. Negation contributes no tokens and is
non-nullable. So skipping their subtrees in FIRST/FOLLOW is fine.

### FIRST/NULLABLE fixed point

After collecting all nodes, initialize FIRST(n)=empty set, NULLABLE(n)=false for all. Then iterate:

```go
for changed {
  changed = false
  for _, n := range nodes {
    switch x := n.(type) {
    case *literal:
      // fixed
    case *reference:
      // fixed
    case *sequence:
      first, nullable := firstSequence(x)
      changed = updateFirst(n, first, &nullable)
    ...
    }
  }
}
```

For `firstSequence`, iterate nodes: for each element, add FIRST(elem) to result; if not nullable, break; else continue. If all nullable,
nullable=true.

For group:
- zeroOrOne/zeroOrMore: first=FIRST(expr); nullable=true.
- once: first=FIRST(expr); nullable=NULLABLE(expr).
- oneOrMore: first=FIRST(expr); nullable=NULLABLE(expr).
- nonEmpty: first=FIRST(expr); nullable=false.

Wait for oneOrMore nullable: if expr nullable, then the group can effectively match empty? As discussed, runtime would error, but for first/follow
detection let's set nullable=true so the group's first propagates through sequences and potential first/follow conflicts are flagged. Actually, hmm —
if `@Ident+` where Ident not nullable, nullable=false. If expr is nullable, then + is problematic; setting nullable=true may cause more first/follow
conflicts. I think setting nullable=NULLABLE(expr) is right.

For disjunction: first = union of FIRST(children); nullable = any child nullable.

For capture/strct: first=FIRST(child); nullable=NULLABLE(child).

For union: first=union of FIRST(members); nullable=any.

For lookaheadGroup: first=∅; nullable=true.

For negation/custom/parseable: first=∅; nullable=false.

Now, iteration order and convergence. Since FIRST only grows and NULLABLE only grows (false→true), it converges. With cycles, e.g. recursive `Expr =
Atom | Expr "+" Atom` — Expr is a strct whose expr includes a capture to Expr itself (via @@). The strct's first set includes Atom; also includes
FIRST(Expr) but only after Atom? Wait `Expr = Atom | Expr "+" Atom` — left recursion, which validate() rejects. So no left-recursive cycles. But there
can be non-left-recursive cycles: `A = "a" B; B = "b" A` — no problem, first sets finite.

What about indirect left recursion through nullable? validate() checks left recursion via strct references in head positions. It might not catch all,
but analysis can still handle with fixpoint.

### FOLLOW fixed point

Initialize FOLLOW(n) = empty for all, FOLLOW(root) = {EOF marker}.

Iterate:
```go
for changed {
  for _, n := range nodes {
    switch x := n.(type) {
    case *sequence:
      elems := flattenSequence(x)
      for i, elem := range elems {
        // tail first: FIRST(elems[i+1:]) with nullable propagation, then FOLLOW(seq) if all tail nullable
        tail := firstOfSequenceFrom(elems, i+1) // set
        addToFollow(elem, tail)
        if sequenceTailAllNullable(elems, i+1) {
          addToFollow(elem, FOLLOW(x))
        }
      }
    case *disjunction:
      for _, c := range x.nodes { addToFollow(c, FOLLOW(x)) }
    case *group:
      addToFollow(x.expr, FOLLOW(x))
    case *capture:
      addToFollow(x.node, FOLLOW(x))
    case *strct:
      addToFollow(x.expr, FOLLOW(x))
    case *union:
      for _, m := range x.disjunction.nodes { addToFollow(m, FOLLOW(x)) }
    }
  }
}
```

Wait, for `strct` as a child node: When a strct node appears in the tree (via capture), it's a node. Its expr is the grammar. FOLLOW(strct) is
computed from its parent sequence. Then the rule `addToFollow(x.expr, FOLLOW(x))` propagates into the struct body. Good.

But there's a subtle issue: `union` node embeds disjunction; the union node itself appears as a capture child. FOLLOW(union) propagates to each
member. Good.

For lookaheadGroup: skip (it's nullable, consumes nothing; its expr is suppressed). But should FOLLOW(lookaheadGroup) propagate? No, because its expr
is not part of consumption. Actually a lookahead group does consume nothing, so following tokens are directly after it; but for the *enclosing*
sequence, the lookahead group's own "follow" is the same as the sequence's follow. Since the lookahead group is nullable, the sequence's first
propagation already handles it (the next element's first goes into the sequence's first). We don't need to propagate into lookahead's expr. Skip.

For negation: skip.

Now the EOF marker: I'll add a special firstToken{kind: eofKind} to FOLLOW(root). For first/follow, FIRST(expr) never contains EOF
(literals/references can't be EOF), so no false positives.

Now, for `firstOfSequenceFrom(elems, start)` — compute union of FIRST(elems[start]), then if nullable, add FIRST(elems[start+1]), etc. Return set.

For sequenceTailAllNullable — all elements from start are nullable.

I need a way to flatten a sequence: `[]node` from head to tail.

### Conflict detection traversal

Now I'll do a separate recursive walk that computes conflict detection, while maintaining currentType/currentField. For each node:

- If `*disjunction`: check first/first among alternatives; check unreachable among alternatives. Then recurse into children with same location.
- If `*group` (mode != once? Actually any group including once? The rule says ?/*/+; groupMatchOnce shouldn't produce first/follow. But also
groupMatchNonEmpty is a modifier — should we check it? The rule says "`?`, `*`, AND `+` groups". So only zeroOrOne, zeroOrMore, oneOrMore. NonEmpty
(groupMatchNonEmpty) is not listed, so skip first/follow for it. But a nonEmpty group is non-nullable, so no first/follow anyway.) For ?/*/+, compute
conflict if FIRST(expr) ∩ FOLLOW(group) ≠ ∅.
- If `*strct`: set currentType = typ name; recurse into expr; restore.
- If `*capture`: set currentField = field.Name; recurse into node; restore.
- If `*union`: recurse into members.
- If `*sequence`: recurse into each element.
- If `*lookaheadGroup` or `*negation`: skip subtree.
- Others: skip.

Wait, for the disjunction inside a strct, currentType should already be set when we enter the strct. For root, set before starting.

But there's a nuance for nested structs via `@@`: when we encounter a `*capture` whose node is a `*strct`, we set field (outer field name), then
recurse into strct which sets type to inner type. When conflicts inside inner, currentField should be inner fields, not the outer field. Since capture
inside inner will set its own field. But when we first enter inner strct, currentField is still the outer capture's field (e.g. field "X" of Outer
with `@@Inner`). If a conflict occurs directly on inner's top-level disjunction (not under a capture), location = {Inner, "X"} — that would be wrong;
it should be {Inner, ""} or the actual inner field. Hmm.

Let me handle: when entering a `*strct`, reset currentField to "" (because the struct's fields will be set by captures inside). Actually, consider
inner struct `Inner { A string \`"a" | "b"\` }`. The top-level disjunction in Inner is not under a capture, so field should be "A"? It's hard to know.
The spec allows "TypeName" without field. So resetting to "" is acceptable: location {Inner, ""}.

But wait — when we set currentType for inner, the capture that wraps it already set currentField to outer field name. If we don't reset, conflicts in
Inner would have wrong field. So reset currentField="" when entering strct. Then captures inside Inner set the field.

Actually, let me reconsider: is `*strct` always reached via a capture? The root strct yes (no capture). Nested strct via `@@` is wrapped in capture.
Union members are strct nodes reached via union (not capture). So resetting on strct entry is the right approach.

Now, how do we know a capture's node is a strct? We just recurse; when we hit strct, reset.

Let me write pseudo:
```go
func analyzeNode(n node, ctx *analysisContext, typeName, fieldName string) {
  switch x := n.(type) {
  case *strct:
     typeName = typeNameOf(x.typ)
     fieldName = ""
     analyzeNode(x.expr, ctx, typeName, fieldName)
  case *capture:
     analyzeNode(x.node, ctx, typeName, x.field.Name)
  case *disjunction:
     checkFirstFirst(x, ctx, typeName, fieldName)
     checkUnreachable(x, ctx, typeName, fieldName)
     for _, c := range x.nodes { analyzeNode(c, ctx, typeName, fieldName) }
  case *group:
     checkFirstFollow(x, ctx, typeName, fieldName)
     analyzeNode(x.expr, ctx, typeName, fieldName)
  case *union:
     for _, m := range x.disjunction.nodes { analyzeNode(m, ctx, typeName, fieldName) }
  case *sequence:
     for c := x; c != nil; c = c.next { analyzeNode(c.node, ctx, typeName, fieldName) }
  case *lookaheadGroup, *negation:
     // skip
  }
}
```

This gives innermost struct type. Good.

Now `typeNameOf`: `t.Name()`; if empty (anonymous), use `t.String()` (e.g. "struct { A string ... }"). The spec says "Go struct type name", so Name()
is preferred. Hidden tests likely use named types.

### FIRST/FIRST detection details

For a disjunction with alternatives a0..an:
- For i<j:

first one). Message includes the overlapping tokens and maybe the alternatives.
  - Also check unreachable: if FIRST_tokens(ai)==FIRST_tokens(aj) AND nullable(ai)==nullable(aj) AND ebnf(ai)==ebnf(aj) → unreachable conflict.

Wait, "identical first sets" — should include nullable? Let me define firstSetsEqual = token sets equal AND nullable equal. For `@Ident | @Ident`,
both {Ident}, nullable false → equal. Good.

conflict per disjunction if any overlap, rather than per pair, to avoid duplicate spam. But maybe per pair is more informative. Hidden tests likely
just check that at least one conflict of the right type exists and count. Hmm, the `ConflictCount` method and Summary count matters. Let me think
about what's most natural: one conflict per pair? For `@Ident | @Ident | @Ident`, pairs (0,1),(0,2),(1,2) → 3 conflicts. For `@Ident | @String |

### First/follow detection details

For each group with mode in {zeroOrOne, zeroOrMore, oneOrMore}:
- first = FIRST(expr)
- follow = FOLLOW(group)
- overlap = first ∩ follow
- if overlap non-empty → conflict.

But wait: for `@Ident* @Ident`, is the group's FOLLOW really {Ident}? Let's trace: Outer sequence = [group(@Ident*), capture(Ident)]. FOLLOW(group) =
FIRST(tail) = {Ident}. first(expr)={Ident}. overlap → conflict.

For `@Ident? @Ident` at end of root: same. Good.

For `@Ident+ @Ident`: same. Good.

Now what about `@Ident+` alone at root? FOLLOW(group) = FOLLOW(root) = {EOF}. first={Ident}. no overlap. Good — no conflict.

What about `@Ident*` followed by `@String` where FIRST(expr)={Ident}, FOLLOW={String} → no conflict. Good.

Now consider nested: `("a"? "b")*` — inside the group expr, there's a sequence [group("a"?), literal("b")]. The inner group "a"? has FOLLOW =
FIRST("b") = {"b"}, first={"a"} → no conflict. Outer * group: first of expr = FIRST(sequence) = {"a"} (since "a"? nullable, "b" follows → first

context. Fine.

Ident?) = {Ident}, nullable=true. Outer sequence = [capture(Inner), capture(Ident)]. FOLLOW(capture(Inner)) = FIRST(tail) = {Ident}. Propagates:
FOLLOW(Inner)=FOLLOW(capture(Inner))={Ident}; then within Inner, FOLLOW(group)=FOLLOW(capture(Ident?))... wait let's trace inner: Inner.expr =
capture(field A, group Ident?). FOLLOW(capture A) = FOLLOW(Inner) = {Ident}. FOLLOW(group) = FOLLOW(capture A) = {Ident}. first(group)= {Ident}.
overlap → conflict at group with location {Inner, "A"}.

### Suppressing lookahead/negation

"Lookahead groups suppress detection in their subtree." So if a conflict would be detected inside a lookahead group, it's suppressed. Our traversal
skips lookaheadGroup children. Also for FIRST/FOLLOW we skip them (treat as nullable empty). Good.

"Negation nodes produce no conflicts." Our traversal skips negation children. Also negation itself produces no first tokens. Good.

### AnalysisReport dedup / Merge

Dedup by (Type, Location.String(), GrammarSnippet). Merge combines and dedups. Both return new values, never mutate. So:
- `Dedup()` returns a new report with unique conflicts by key, preserving first occurrence order.
- `Merge(other)` returns a new report = current conflicts + other conflicts, deduplicated.

`FilterByType` returns new report with only that type. `FilterWith` new report with predicate. `Errors()` and `Warnings()` return slices (new).
`IsClean()` = len(Conflicts)==0.

`ConflictCount(t)` count by type. `HasType(t)` count>0.

### SuppressConflictType

`SuppressConflictType(t ConflictType) AnalysisOption` — an option that filters conflicts of that type from the report. So
`AnalyzeWithOptions(SuppressConflictType(ConflictFirstFollow))` returns report without first/follow conflicts. Implement as an `AnalysisOption`
function that holds a set of suppressed types; after analysis, filter them out.

How is `AnalysisOption` defined? It's an analyze-tagged type. Could be `type AnalysisOption func(*analysisOptions)`. Let me define:
```go
type AnalysisOption func(*analysisOptions)
type analysisOptions struct { suppressed map[ConflictType]bool }
func SuppressConflictType(t ConflictType) AnalysisOption { ... }
func (p *Parser[G]) AnalyzeWithOptions(opts ...AnalysisOption) (*AnalysisReport, error) {
   o := &analysisOptions{}
   for _, opt := range opts { opt(o) }
   report, err := p.analyze()
   if err != nil { return nil, err }
   if len(o.suppressed) > 0 { report = report.FilterWith(func(c Conflict) bool { return !o.suppressed[c.Type] }) }
   return report, nil
}
```

`Analyze()` calls `AnalyzeWithOptions()`.

`strictAnalyze()` — defined in analyze-tagged file and !analyze stub. But wait: the method name `strictAnalyze` is unexported. Both files define it.
The analyze-tagged version:
```go
func (p *Parser[G]) strictAnalyze() error {
    report, err := p.analyze()
    if err != nil { return err }
    if !report.IsClean() {
        return fmt.Errorf("grammar conflicts detected: %s", report.Summary())

}
```

### Analysis implementation details

Let me write the analysis code now. I'll structure it:

```go
//go:build analyze

package participle

import (
    "fmt"
    "reflect"
    "sort"
    "strings"
)

// Types...

type ConflictType int
const (
    ConflictFirstFirst ConflictType = iota
    ConflictFirstFollow
    ConflictUnreachable
)
func (t ConflictType) String() string { ... }

type Severity int
const (SeverityWarning, SeverityError)
func (s Severity) String() string { ... }

type ConflictLocation struct { TypeName, FieldName string }
func (l ConflictLocation) String() string { if l.FieldName == "" { return l.TypeName }; return l.TypeName + "." + l.FieldName }

type Conflict struct {
    Type ConflictType
    Severity Severity
    Message string
    Location ConflictLocation
    GrammarSnippet string
    Example string
    Suggestion string
}
func (c Conflict) String() string {
    return fmt.Sprintf("[%s] %s at %s: %s", c.Severity, c.Type, c.Location, c.Message)
}

type AnalysisReport struct { Conflicts []Conflict }
// methods...
```

Now the analysis engine:

```go
type analysis struct {
    nodes []node
    first map[node]map[firstToken]bool
    nullable map[node]bool
    follow map[node]map[firstToken]bool
}
```

`firstToken`:
```go
type firstToken struct {
    kind firstKind
    value string
}
type firstKind int
const (
    firstLiteral firstKind = iota
    firstType
    firstEOF
)
func (f firstToken) String() string {
    switch f.kind {
    case firstLiteral: return fmt.Sprintf("%q", f.value)
    case firstType: return "<" + strings.ToLower(f.value) + ">"
    case firstEOF: return "<eof>"
    }
}
```

Wait, for type names, reference.identifier is e.g. "Ident". Display as `<ident>`. Good.

Let me define first set helpers:
```go
func (a *analysis) firstSet(n node) map[firstToken]bool { return a.first[n] }
func (a *analysis) isNullable(n node) bool { return a.nullable[n] }
```

Now collect nodes:
```go
func collectNodes(root node) []node {
    seen := map[node]bool{}
    out := []node{}
    var walk func(node)
    walk = func(n node) {
        if n == nil || seen[n] { return }
        seen[n] = true
        out = append(out, n)
        switch x := n.(type) {
        case *disjunction:
            for _, c := range x.nodes { walk(c) }
        case *strct:
            walk(x.expr)
        case *union:
            for _, m := range x.disjunction.nodes { walk(m) }
        case *sequence:
            for c := x; c != nil; c = c.next { walk(c.node) }
        case *capture:
            walk(x.node)
        case *group:
            walk(x.expr)
        case *lookaheadGroup:
        case *negation:
        }
    }
    walk(root)
    return out
}
```

Wait — should collectNodes include strct nodes reached through captures? Yes, because capture walks x.node which may be *strct. Good.

Now first/nullable computation. Need `flattenSequence` and helpers:
```go
func sequenceNodes(s *sequence) []node {
    var out []node
    for ; s != nil; s = s.next { out = append(out, s.node) }
    return out
}
```

first of sequence tail:
```go
func (a *analysis) firstOfList(nodes []node) (map[firstToken]bool, bool) {
    out := map[firstToken]bool{}
    allNullable := true
    for _, n := range nodes {
        for f := range a.first[n] { out[f] = true }
        if !a.nullable[n] { allNullable = false; break }
    }
    return out, allNullable
}
```
Actually allNullable should be true only if ALL nodes nullable; the loop above sets false if any non-nullable, but if we break early, the remaining
nodes aren't considered — that's fine because allNullable only matters if we got through all. But if we break on first non-nullable, allNullable=false
and remaining don't matter. Correct.

Update logic per node:
```go
func (a *analysis) computeFirst(n node) (map[firstToken]bool, bool) {
    switch x := n.(type) {
    case *literal:
        if x.t == lexer.EOF {
            return map[firstToken]bool{{firstLiteral, x.s}: true}, false
        }
        return map[firstToken]bool{{firstType, x.tt}: true}, false
    case *reference:
        return map[firstToken]bool{{firstType, x.identifier}: true}, false
    case *sequence:
        return a.firstOfList(sequenceNodes(x))
    case *disjunction:
        out := map[firstToken]bool{}
        nullable := false
        for _, c := range x.nodes {
            for f := range a.first[c] { out[f] = true }
            if a.nullable[c] { nullable = true }
        }
        return out, nullable
    case *group:
        switch x.mode {
        case groupMatchZeroOrOne, groupMatchZeroOrMore:
            out := map[firstToken]bool{}
            for f := range a.first[x.expr] { out[f] = true }
            return out, true
        case groupMatchNonEmpty:
            out := map[firstToken]bool{}
            for f := range a.first[x.expr] { out[f] = true }
            return out, false
        default: // once, oneOrMore
            out := map[firstToken]bool{}
            for f := range a.first[x.expr] { out[f] = true }
            return out, a.nullable[x.expr]
        }
    case *capture:
        return a.copyFirst(x.node), a.nullable[x.node]
    case *strct:
        return a.copyFirst(x.expr), a.nullable[x.expr]
    case *union:
        out := map[firstToken]bool{}
        nullable := false
        for _, m := range x.disjunction.nodes {
            for f := range a.first[m] { out[f] = true }
            if a.nullable[m] { nullable = true }
        }
        return out, nullable
    case *lookaheadGroup:
        return map[firstToken]bool{}, true
    case *negation, *custom, *parseable:
        return map[firstToken]bool{}, false
    }
    return nil, false
}
```

Note: for `literal` with type constraint `x.t != EOF`, I treat as firstType with x.tt. If `x.tt` is empty (shouldn't be when constrained), fallback.
Also `literal` without constraint uses firstLiteral.

Hmm wait, there's an issue: `literal.s` can be empty string (e.g. `""`). Then firstToken value "" — firstLiteral. That's fine.

Now fixed-point:
```go
func (a *analysis) computeFirstSets() {
    for _, n := range a.nodes {
        a.first[n] = map[firstToken]bool{}
        a.nullable[n] = false
    }
    for {
        changed := false
        for _, n := range a.nodes {
            first, nullable := a.computeFirst(n)
            if !setEqual(a.first[n], first) { a.first[n] = first; changed = true }
            if a.nullable[n] != nullable { a.nullable[n] = nullable; changed = true }
        }
        if !changed { break }
    }
}
```

Wait, `computeFirst(n)` references `a.first[child]` which may not be updated yet in this pass — that's fine for fixed point; we iterate until stable.
But there's a subtle bug: `computeFirst` for `*sequence` uses `a.firstOfList` which reads `a.first[n]` for children — all initialized. Good.

For `*group` oneOrMore nullable = `a.nullable[x.expr]`. Good.

Now FOLLOW computation. Initialize follow[n] = empty, follow[root] = {EOF}. Iterate:
```go
func (a *analysis) computeFollowSets(root node) {
    for _, n := range a.nodes { a.follow[n] = map[firstToken]bool{} }
    a.follow[root][firstToken{firstEOF, ""}] = true
    for {
        changed := false
        for _, n := range a.nodes {
            switch x := n.(type) {
            case *sequence:
                nodes := sequenceNodes(x)
                for i, child := range nodes {
                    tail, allNullable := a.firstOfList(nodes[i+1:])
                    if a.addFollow(child, tail) { changed = true }
                    if allNullable {
                        if a.addFollow(child, a.follow[x]) { changed = true }
                    }
                }
            case *disjunction:
                for _, c := range x.nodes {
                    if a.addFollow(c, a.follow[x]) { changed = true }
                }
            case *group:
                if a.addFollow(x.expr, a.follow[x]) { changed = true }
            case *capture:
                if a.addFollow(x.node, a.follow[x]) { changed = true }
            case *strct:
                if a.addFollow(x.expr, a.follow[x]) { changed = true }
            case *union:
                for _, m := range x.disjunction.nodes {
                    if a.addFollow(m, a.follow[x]) { changed = true }
                }
            }
        }
        if !changed { break }
    }
}
```

Wait — there's a subtle issue with `firstOfList` returning `allNullable=true` for an empty tail. For the last element of a sequence, tail is empty →
allNullable=true → add FOLLOW(seq) to last element. Good. But for firstOfList(empty) returns empty set, allNullable=true. Good.

But there's another subtle issue: `firstOfList` reads `a.first[child]` which might be nil if child not in nodes. All children are in nodes
(collected). Good.

Now, the `addFollow` method adds elements of `src` into `a.follow[dst]` and returns whether changed.

Potential issue: recursion and convergence with cycles. Standard. Should converge since follows only grow.

But wait — there's a subtlety for strct follow propagation with cycles: If strct A's expr contains a capture to strct A (recursion), then follow of A
propagates into A's expr, and A's expr's last elements get FOLLOW(A). This can loop but converges. Good.

Now, one more subtlety for first/follow detection: We detect on group nodes. But what about a `?` group that is the expr of a `+` group, etc.? It's
fine.

Now, the conflict detection traversal. I'll write:

```go
func (a *analysis) detect(n node) []Conflict {
    var conflicts []Conflict
    var walk func(node, string, string)
    walk = func(n node, typeName, fieldName string) {
        switch x := n.(type) {
        case *strct:
            walk(x.expr, typeNameOf(x.typ), "")
        case *capture:
            walk(x.node, typeName, x.field.Name)
        case *disjunction:
            conflicts = append(conflicts, a.detectDisjunction(x, typeName, fieldName)...)
            for _, c := range x.nodes { walk(c, typeName, fieldName) }
        case *group:
            if x.mode == groupMatchZeroOrOne || x.mode == groupMatchZeroOrMore || x.mode == groupMatchOneOrMore {
                if c := a.detectFirstFollow(x, typeName, fieldName); c != nil {
                    conflicts = append(conflicts, *c)
                }
            }
            walk(x.expr, typeName, fieldName)
        case *union:
            for _, m := range x.disjunction.nodes { walk(m, typeName, fieldName) }
        case *sequence:
            for c := x; c != nil; c = c.next { walk(c.node, typeName, fieldName) }
        case *lookaheadGroup, *negation:
            // suppressed
        }
    }
    walk(n, typeNameOf(n.(*strct).typ), "")  // root is strct
    return conflicts
}
```

Wait — root is always a *strct? In `Build`, rootNode from parseType(rootType) is a *strct (or union if root is interface?). Actually rootType could be
interface? Build: `v := reflect.ValueOf(&grammar)`; if grammar is an interface, v.Elem()... hmm root type could be interface type if G is an
interface? `var grammar G; v := reflect.ValueOf(&grammar); if v.Kind() == reflect.Interface { v = v.Elem() }; p.rootType = v.Type()`. If G is
interface, rootType is the interface's dynamic type? Actually `&grammar` where grammar is interface gives *interface. v.Kind()==Ptr, not Interface.
Hmm. Not important; root node from parseType is usually *strct. But to be safe, handle root generically:

```go
walk(n, typeNameOfRoot(n), "")
```
where typeNameOfRoot returns the root's type if strct. Let me just check `if s, ok := n.(*strct); ok { typeName = typeNameOf(s.typ) }`. For other root
types, typeName = "".

Actually `parseType` can return union node if G is an interface and Union option is used? Let me not worry; handle gracefully.

`detectDisjunction`:
```go
func (a *analysis) detectDisjunction(d *disjunction, typeName, fieldName string) []Conflict {
    var out []Conflict
    for i := 0; i < len(d.nodes); i++ {
        for j := i + 1; j < len(d.nodes); j++ {
            left, right := d.nodes[i], d.nodes[j]
            overlap := intersect(a.first[left], a.first[right])
            if len(overlap) > 0 {
                out = append(out, a.firstFirstConflict(d, left, right, overlap, typeName, fieldName))
            }
            if firstSetsEqual(left, right) && ebnf(left) == ebnf(right) {
                out = append(out, a.unreachableConflict(d, right, left, typeName, fieldName))
            }
        }
    }
    return out
}
```

Where firstSetsEqual compares token first sets AND nullable. `ebnf(left)` uses existing ebnf function.

Wait — if first sets are equal AND nullable equal AND ebnf equal, then there's also an overlap (if first set non-empty) → both first/first and
unreachable. If first sets are both empty (both nullable only), no overlap but unreachable fires (if ebnf equal). E.g. `@Ident? | @Ident?` — first
sets {Ident}, overlap → first/first AND unreachable. `"a"? | "b"?` — first sets {"a"} vs {"b"}, not equal, no overlap; not unreachable, no
first/first. But as I discussed, `"a"? | "b"?` second alt is actually unreachable at runtime (first matches empty always). But spec says unreachable
requires identical first sets AND identical EBNF; they're not identical, so no unreachable. Fine.

Hmm, but should `"a"? | "b"?` trigger first/first? No overlap (a vs b) → no. OK.

For `@Ident? | @Ident?`, first sets both {Ident}, nullable both true, ebnf `<ident>?` == `<ident>?` → unreachable. And first/first (overlap Ident).
Both fire. That seems right per rules.

Now, message/example/suggestion construction:

```go
func (a *analysis) firstFirstConflict(d *disjunction, left, right node, overlap map[firstToken]bool, typeName, fieldName string) Conflict {
    tokens := sortedFirstTokens(overlap)
    return Conflict{
        Type: ConflictFirstFirst,
        Severity: SeverityWarning,
        Message: fmt.Sprintf("alternatives share overlapping first token(s): %s", joinTokens(tokens)),
        Location: ConflictLocation{typeName, fieldName},
        GrammarSnippet: d.String(), // ebnf
        Example: joinTokens(tokens),
        Suggestion: "Add lookahead or reorder alternatives to remove the overlap",
    }
}
```

`joinTokens` for example: join token strings with space. For literal, `"a"`; for type `<ident>`. "concrete token sequence" — e.g. `<ident>` or `"a"`.
Good.

`unreachableConflict`:
```go
Conflict{
  Type: ConflictUnreachable,
  Severity: SeverityError,
  Message: fmt.Sprintf("alternative %s is shadowed by earlier identical alternative %s", ebnf(right), ebnf(left)),
  Location: ...,
  GrammarSnippet: d.String(),
  Example: joinTokens(sortedFirstTokens(a.first[left])), // first token
  Suggestion: "Remove the unreachable alternative",
}
```
Example must be non-empty. If first sets empty (both nullable), joinTokens returns "" → need fallback. For `@Ident? | @Ident?` first={Ident} fine. For
a purely epsilon alternative, e.g. `(@Ident?)? | (@Ident?)?` first={Ident}, fine. Could there be an alternative with empty first set and nullable
(like `(?= "a")` lookahead)? Lookahead groups are suppressed but could be alternatives: `(?= "a") | (?= "a")` — first sets empty, nullable true, ebnf
`(?= "a")` equal → unreachable. Example empty. I'll fallback to `"<epsilon>"` when empty. Good — keeps non-empty.

`firstFollowConflict`:
```go
func (a *analysis) detectFirstFollow(g *group, typeName, fieldName string) *Conflict {
    overlap := intersect(a.first[g.expr], a.follow[g])
    if len(overlap) == 0 { return nil }
    tokens := sortedFirstTokens(overlap)
    return &Conflict{
        Type: ConflictFirstFollow,
        Severity: SeverityWarning,
        Message: fmt.Sprintf("group %s can match a token that can also follow it: %s", g.String(), joinTokens(tokens)),
        Location: ...,
        GrammarSnippet: g.String(),
        Example: joinTokens(tokens),
        Suggestion: "Add a delimiter or lookahead to disambiguate the repetition boundary",
    }
}
```

Wait — for `@Ident+ @Ident`, the group's follow is {Ident}. But the group String() is `<ident>+`. Good.

Now, let me reconsider the first/follow rule: "`?`, `*`, AND `+` groups whose first tokens overlap the follow set." What about a `?` group inside a
disjunction where the disjunction itself is in a follow context? E.g. `@Ident? @Ident` — group at root, follow {Ident}. Good.

But there's an important subtlety: should first/follow also consider the *nullable* of expr? For `@Ident? @Ident`, expr first={Ident}. For `(@Ident?
@String)? @Ident` — the outer ? group's expr first = {Ident} (since inner `@Ident?` nullable, first includes Ident and String). Follow = {Ident}.
Conflict. That's correct.

Now another subtlety: the follow set of a group should perhaps also include tokens that can follow due to the group being repeated. E.g. `(@Ident?)*
@Ident`. The outer * group's expr is a sequence [group(Ident?), literal... wait no, the expr of outer * is the inner `@Ident?` sequence? Let me not
overcomplicate; standard FOLLOW handles it.

Hmm, wait — there IS a case where the self-repetition contributes to follow ambiguity: Consider `@Ident* @Ident*`. First group's FOLLOW = FIRST(second
group) = {Ident} → conflict. Good. What about `(@Ident @Ident*)? @Ident`? Inner * group's FOLLOW: inside sequence [Ident, group*], FOLLOW(group*) =
FOLLOW(seq) = FOLLOW(outer?) = {Ident}. first(expr)=Ident → conflict at inner * group. Good.

Now, there's another interpretation issue: For `+` groups, the spec says "whose first tokens overlap the follow set." For `@Ident+ @Ident`, yes. But
should we also check `@Ident+` where FOLLOW includes FIRST(expr) due to repetition (i.e., the boundary between iterations)? I argued no because
there's no real choice. But consider `@Ident+ @Ident` — the parser after matching some Idents sees Ident; it could continue the loop OR stop. Both
lead to valid full parses (loop consumes all Idents, or stops and following capture consumes some Idents, leaving rest? Wait if it stops and following
@Ident consumes one Ident, then remaining Idents are unconsumed → parse error unless trailing allowed. Actually the parser's group loop: it tries
expr; if matches, continues greedily. So it will consume all Idents in the loop, then following @Ident fails at EOF → overall parse fails. If it
stopped early, following @Ident consumes one, but the group would then... no, the group already tried and matched, it doesn't "stop early" unless expr
fails. Hmm. Let me think about how the parser actually behaves for `@Ident+ @Ident` on input "a b".

Actually the ambiguity: parser parses `@Ident+` greedily. With lookahead, after matching "a", it sees "b". It tries expr (Ident) on "b" → matches,
consumes "b", continues. Then at EOF, tries expr → fails, loop ends with 2 Idents consumed. Then following @Ident at EOF fails → overall parse error.
But an alternative parse would be: `@Ident+` matches only "a", then following @Ident matches "b" → success. So the grammar is ambiguous: input "a b"
can be parsed two ways, one successful one not. This is exactly first/follow. So the conflict is real. Good — our detection catches it.

For `@Ident+` alone on "a b": parser consumes all Idents, then succeeds at EOF (if b is Ident too). No ambiguity — the loop greedily consumes all;
there's no following node competing. So no conflict. Good.

Now, what about `@Ident* @Ident` — same as +. Good.

Now let me think about whether first/follow should check `groupMatchNonEmpty`. E.g. `@Ident! @Ident` — nonEmpty is not nullable, so no ambiguity (it
always matches exactly one Ident). FOLLOW(expr) after group = {Ident}, but expr first={Ident} and group isn't in ?/*/+ list → no conflict. Our code
skips nonEmpty. Good.

### Type name extraction

```go
func typeNameOf(t reflect.Type) string {
    if t.Name() != "" { return t.Name() }
    return t.String()
}
```

### Sorted first tokens for deterministic output

### Now, the `analyze()` method

```go
func (p *Parser[G]) analyze() (*AnalysisReport, error) {
    root := p.typeNodes[p.rootType]
    if root == nil {
        return nil, fmt.Errorf("parser has no grammar")
    }
    a := newAnalysis(root)
    a.computeFirstSets()
    a.computeFollowSets(root)
    conflicts := a.detect(root)
    return &AnalysisReport{Conflicts: conflicts}, nil
}
```

Wait — need to be careful: `p.typeNodes[p.rootType]` — rootType is set in Build. But for parsers built via ParserForProduction, rootType remains the
original root? Let me check ParserForProduction: returns (*Parser[P])(parser) — casts, so rootType is still original G's root, but typeNodes includes
it. Fine. Analyze on a production parser would analyze the whole G grammar, which is acceptable.

Actually, there's a subtlety: Build sets `p.rootType = v.Type()` where v is reflect.ValueOf(&grammar). Let me check: `v := reflect.ValueOf(&grammar)`;
grammar is type G. `&grammar` is *G. v.Kind()==Ptr. p.rootType = v.Type() = *G (ptr type). Then rootNode, err := context.parseType(p.rootType).
parseType calls indirectType(t) which derefs ptr → G. Returns strct node. p.typeNodes[p.rootType] = rootNode. Wait, but parseType stores typeNodes[t]
where t is the indirected type (G struct), not *G. Then Build does `p.typeNodes[p.rootType] = rootNode` — p.rootType is *G. So typeNodes[*G] =
rootNode too. In analyze, `p.typeNodes[p.rootType]` gets rootNode. Good.

But hold on — `p.rootType` is `*G`, and `typeNameOf` on *G → Name() empty (ptr type has no name), String() = "*main.Grammar". I need to indirect:
`typeNameOf(t.Elem())` or use the strct node's typ. In detect, when walking root strct, I use `s.typ` which is the struct type G (parseType stored G
as strct.typ). So I'll set root type from the strct node's typ, not p.rootType. Good.

### StrictMode integration details

In Build, after setting typeNodes:
```go
p.typeNodes = context.typeNodes
p.typeNodes[p.rootType] = rootNode
p.setCaseInsensitiveTokens()
if p.strictMode {
    if err := p.strictAnalyze(); err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }
}
return p, nil
```

Wait, but strictAnalyze is a method on *Parser[G], and Build's receiver is `p *Parser[G]`. Good.

Now, `strictAnalyze` in analyze-tagged file calls `p.analyze()`. In !analyze file returns nil.

### The `AnalysisReport` methods

Let me write them:
```go
func (r *AnalysisReport) Errors() []Conflict {
    var out []Conflict
    for _, c := range r.Conflicts { if c.Severity == SeverityError { out = append(out, c) } }
    return out
}
func (r *AnalysisReport) Warnings() []Conflict {
    var out []Conflict
    for _, c := range r.Conflicts { if c.Severity == SeverityWarning { out = append(out, c) } }
    return out
}
func (r *AnalysisReport) FilterByType(t ConflictType) *AnalysisReport {
    return r.FilterWith(func(c Conflict) bool { return c.Type == t })
}
func (r *AnalysisReport) FilterWith(fn func(Conflict) bool) *AnalysisReport {
    out := &AnalysisReport{}
    for _, c := range r.Conflicts { if fn(c) { out.Conflicts = append(out.Conflicts, c) } }
    return out
}
func (r *AnalysisReport) ConflictCount(t ConflictType) int {
    n := 0
    for _, c := range r.Conflicts { if c.Type == t { n++ } }
    return n
}
func (r *AnalysisReport) HasType(t ConflictType) bool { return r.ConflictCount(t) > 0 }
func (r *AnalysisReport) IsClean() bool { return len(r.Conflicts) == 0 }
func (r *AnalysisReport) Summary() string {
    if r.IsClean() { return "no conflicts detected" }
    return fmt.Sprintf("%d conflict(s): %d first/first, %d first/follow, %d unreachable",
        len(r.Conflicts), r.ConflictCount(ConflictFirstFirst), r.ConflictCount(ConflictFirstFollow), r.ConflictCount(ConflictUnreachable))
}
func (r *AnalysisReport) String() string {
    if r.IsClean() { return "no conflicts detected" }
    parts := []string{fmt.Sprintf("%d conflict(s)", len(r.Conflicts))}
    for _, c := range r.Conflicts {
        parts = append(parts, c.String())
    }
    return strings.Join(parts, "\n")
}
func (r *AnalysisReport) Merge(other *AnalysisReport) *AnalysisReport {
    merged := &AnalysisReport{}
    merged.Conflicts = append(merged.Conflicts, r.Conflicts...)
    if other != nil { merged.Conflicts = append(merged.Conflicts, other.Conflicts...) }
    return merged.Dedup()
}
func conflictKey(c Conflict) string { return fmt.Sprintf("%d|%s|%s", c.Type, c.Location.String(), c.GrammarSnippet) }
func (r *AnalysisReport) Dedup() *AnalysisReport {
    out := &AnalysisReport{}
    seen := map[string]bool{}
    for _, c := range r.Conflicts {
        k := conflictKey(c)
        if !seen[k] { seen[k] = true; out.Conflicts = append(out.Conflicts, c) }
    }
    return out
}
```

Wait — "Merge combine + deduplicate by (Type, Location.String(), GrammarSnippet)". My Dedup uses exactly that. Good.

`Summary()` exact format: `"N conflict(s): A first/first, B first/follow, C unreachable"`. Note it says "N conflict(s):" with literal "conflict(s)".
I'll use exactly that.

### AnalysisOption / SuppressConflictType

```go
type AnalysisOption func(*analysisOptions)

type analysisOptions struct {
    suppressed map[ConflictType]bool
}

func SuppressConflictType(t ConflictType) AnalysisOption {
    return func(o *analysisOptions) {
        if o.suppressed == nil { o.suppressed = map[ConflictType]bool{} }
        o.suppressed[t] = true
    }
}

func (p *Parser[G]) Analyze() (*AnalysisReport, error) {
    return p.AnalyzeWithOptions()
}

func (p *Parser[G]) AnalyzeWithOptions(opts ...AnalysisOption) (*AnalysisReport, error) {
    o := &analysisOptions{}
    for _, opt := range opts { opt(o) }
    report, err := p.analyze()
    if err != nil { return nil, err }
    if len(o.suppressed) > 0 {
        report = report.FilterWith(func(c Conflict) bool { return !o.suppressed[c.Type] })
    }
    return report, nil
}
```

### Let me also think about `firstOfList` and follow for `*sequence` with `head` field

The `sequence` struct has `head bool`; the first element is head. When flattening, just iterate next pointers. Good.

### Potential issue: `ebnf()` on a node inside a lookahead/negation

We skip those subtrees entirely, so no ebnf calls on suppressed nodes. For snippets on disjunction/group, ebnf works.

### Potential issue: computeFollow for `*strct` and `*union` — these nodes may also appear as children of `capture`. In collectNodes, both the strct
node and its expr are included. The follow propagation: FOLLOW(strct) → FOLLOW(expr). And the sequence containing the capture sets FOLLOW(capture) →
FOLLOW(strct). So FOLLOW(strct) propagates into expr. Good.

But wait — in the conflict detection traversal, when we encounter a `*capture` wrapping a `*strct`, we set fieldName to the outer field, then walk the
strct which resets typeName and fieldName. Good.

### Now let me think about a tricky FIRST computation bug with `*strct`:

`computeFirst` for strct returns `a.copyFirst(x.expr), a.nullable[x.expr]`. But `x.expr` might be nil? For a struct being parsed, expr is set. Good.

### Another tricky case: `union` node in typeNodes

`addUnionDefs` creates unionNode and sets `g.typeNodes[def.typ] = unionNode`. Then `parseType` for an interface field returns the union node from
typeNodes. So a capture's node can be `*union`. In collectNodes, `case *union: for _, m := range x.disjunction.nodes { walk(m) }`. Good. In
computeFirst union handles members. In follow, union propagates to members. In detection, union walks members. Good.

### Let me also consider `*custom` and `*parseable`

These are leaves with no first tokens, not nullable. If a sequence starts with custom, first is empty and non-nullable → the sequence's first is
empty, non-nullable. Then no first/follow conflicts involving custom. That's safe (won't produce false positives). But could miss real conflicts.
Acceptable.

// MaxLookahead can be used with UseLookahead to get pseudo-infinite
// lookahead without the risk of pathological cases causing a stack
// overflow.
const MaxLookahead = 99999

// An Option to modify the behaviour of the Parser.
type Option func(p *parserOptions) error

// Lexer is an Option that sets the lexer to use with the given grammar.
func Lexer(def lexer.Definition) Option {
	return func(p *parserOptions) error {
		p.lex = def
		return nil
	}
}

// UseLookahead allows branch lookahead up to "n" tokens.
//
// If parsing cannot be disambiguated before "n" tokens of lookahead, parsing will fail.
//
// Note that increasing lookahead has a minor performance impact, but also
// reduces the accuracy of error reporting.
//
// If "n" is negative, it will be treated as "infinite" lookahead.
// This can have a large impact on performance, and does not provide any
// protection against stack overflow during parsing.
// In most cases, using MaxLookahead will achieve the same results in practice,
// but with a concrete upper bound to prevent pathological behavior in the parser.
// Using infinite lookahead can be useful for testing, or for parsing especially
// ambiguous grammars. Use at your own risk!
